ParlaMint-EE_2017-06-14_U12	The next question is for Kadri Samson, Minister for Economic Affairs and Infrastructure. Arthur Talvik, please!
ParlaMint-EE_2017-06-14_U13	Thank you, Chairman. Good minister! Rail Baltic's cost-benefit analysis was carried out by a company called EY. Its analysis shows that, in socio-economic terms, Rail Baltic is very profitable. However, Estonian experts have taken these calculations into account and prove that the wrong source data and the socio-economic benefits that EY's analysis shows have been estimated at 4.1 billion. It is known that CEF will not finance projects that are not socio-economically viable. I'm sure you've got the same calculation at the Ministry. What's your move now?
ParlaMint-EE_2017-06-14_U14	Dear Artur Talvik, I thank you for this question. The Ministry of Economic Affairs will certainly take all experts and their calculations very seriously. We therefore made an inquiry to the European Commission as soon as the argument that the cost-benefit analysis carried out by the EY company had been based on the wrong data. Of course, it is important for us that socio-economic analysis be accepted by the European Commission, because without it, the project cannot go any further. We received a preliminary rapid response that the cost-benefit analysis was based on the European Commission's guidance and the initial task of carrying out the work. All these aspects have been assessed on the basis of the European Commission's CBA guide. Similar guidelines are used in Estonia to assess the socio-economic impact of other important infrastructure projects. So this guidance material has been the basis for road projects, but also for water projects, for example. The preliminary confirmation that the cost-benefit analysis of this respected company meets the requirements is, in my opinion, a good way forward.
ParlaMint-EE_2017-06-14_U15	Arthur Talvik, a more specific question, please!
ParlaMint-EE_2017-06-14_U16	Thank you! These initial tasks and the way it was done were certainly correct. But what I'm paying attention to is the information that EY has used the wrong data. For example, old data have been used for the pollution of cars; they are not based on today's cars' pollution volumes. In such a case, should the Government not suspend and postpone ratification of the Rail Baltic Treaty until the disputes have been held?
ParlaMint-EE_2017-06-14_U17	The validation of such necessary quality analyses is subject to quality control. The EY analysis involves experts used by the European Commission's Directorates-General for Mobility, Transport and Regional and Urban Policy to evaluate large-scale transport infrastructure development projects. Now, the question is, as if the allowance had not taken into account which cars will be driving on Estonian roads in the future. It has been a principle that the analysis is based on existing laws and current circumstances. And now we have this situation where heavy goods vehicles are driving on Estonian, Baltic and European roads, which are eight years old in the middle. So let us admit that if the analysis had been made on the assumption that in 2026 all cars on our roads would have been brand new, it would have seemed overstretched rather than a reality-compliant analysis, which says that all heavy goods vehicles on the roads in 2026 are not the newest or the least polluting.
ParlaMint-EE_2017-06-14_U18	Hatter, please!
ParlaMint-EE_2017-06-14_U19	Good Chairman! Good minister! I'll continue with a question I didn't get an answer to on Monday. The National Audit Office points out that the provision of Rail Baltic as a new service is not required by law for the state. I asked you which laws were the state service, because when you answered the question, you said that Rail Baltic was a public service. Please state the laws that say it is a public service.
ParlaMint-EE_2017-06-14_U20	We have certain investments made by the private sector because they are profitable. The state also has certain needs in order for its inhabitants to be able to move and for entrepreneurship and future investment to find a favourable growth environment that private investors do not offer. And then there are two options: whether such investments will not occur in our country, or whether we will invest them for the money of the Estonian state that invested them, or also in combination with European funds. Infrastructure is one of those tasks. We keep up the existing 1520 mm rail infrastructure, we invest in highways, we keep up the ferry connection. Undoubtedly, in the case of Rail Baltic, after we were able to decide in 2014 that the stop is not only in Tallinn, but it will also come to Rapla and Pärnu, we will ensure a rapid rail connection in one region. Now, on the other hand, it is certainly a step that will contribute to our future economic growth. This is not my personal understanding, it is not the understanding of the supporters of Rail Baltic, but it has also been referred to by international rating agencies, the latter being Standard & Poor's, who said that it would certainly support our long-term and continued growth and investment in rail projects. The decisions to build Rail Baltic have been made years before today. The first move, as the National Audit Office showed, was made on behalf of the then Prime Minister Andres Tarand. And with this ratification treaty, which will come to the Riigikogu on Monday, we will not accept any new commitments and will not change the commitments already made. I think this is a pretty important knowledge for people who support the ratification of an international agreement.
ParlaMint-EE_2017-06-14_U21	I will conclude on this issue.
ParlaMint-EE_2017-06-14_U22	Asks Arno Bridge and answers Foreign Minister Sven Mikser. Please, Arno Bridge!
ParlaMint-EE_2017-06-14_U23	Thank you, Mr President-in-Office. Minister, thank you very much. As is well known, there are three European countries - Hungary, Poland and the Czech Republic - with differing views on the problem of receiving refugees. Therefore, my question is what is the position of Estonia, the position of the Estonian Ministry of Foreign Affairs in these problems.
ParlaMint-EE_2017-06-14_U24	Thank you. The question is raised in a relatively general way. As regards the position of the Estonian Government, Estonia has expressed this by participating in the mechanism for the redistribution of refugees and, of course, in all the discussions that preceded the creation of this mechanism. I expect the core of your question to be linked to recent news that the European Commission may launch infringement proceedings against these three countries. Such a right in the case of the Commission is a direct consequence of the underlying agreements in Europe. Article 258 of the consolidated version of the basic agreements states: 'If the Commission considers that a Member State has failed to fulfil an obligation under the Treaties, it shall give a reasoned opinion on this. ' If the State concerned does not comply with that opinion, the Commission may refer the State to the European Court of Justice. This is certainly the Commission's right. There have been quite a number of infringement proceedings against Member States on various issues. These legal disputes have resulted in different results, in some cases the right to the Commission and sometimes the right to a Member State. More generally, it is certainly the principle that the obligations arising from the legislation and in respect of which one or the other party has been critical at the time of their proceedings must also actually be fulfilled. Here, we certainly fully respect this principle, but the internal dimension of migration in terms of the organisation of work of the Estonian government is more in the area of administration of the Ministry of Internal Affairs. On the other hand, as Minister for Foreign Affairs, I look at the possible legal dispute ahead with considerable concern, because it will certainly put considerable pressure on European internal solidarity and unity, which is not in the interests of Estonia as one Member State.
ParlaMint-EE_2017-06-14_U25	Arno Bridge, a more specific question, please!
ParlaMint-EE_2017-06-14_U26	Thank you, Mr President-in-Office. Now, as far as I know, Hungary and Poland have brought this problem to court, but it is not known when this court settlement will come. So how does the European Union behave? Does it want to do something before the court settlement comes? What is your opinion on this? That's one. And secondly, are there any other countries that intend to act like these three, or are there no more?
ParlaMint-EE_2017-06-14_U27	Thank you. First of all, once again, I think you're somewhat wrong about the addressee. The European Union as a whole and, for example, the internal dimension of European migration policy are certainly not foreign policy for the Estonian state. However, I do believe that the internal and external dimensions of migration are, in a certain sense, inseparable, so I am happy to try to answer you. A court dispute as to whether one or the other instrument is binding on a Member State and binding on it or not is certainly a completely legitimate way to determine how European law is to be implemented on one or the other. In this sense, I would certainly not demonise in any way if one or the other Member State, or the Commission, is prepared to refer its opinion or position to the European Court of Justice. I, who have been in this Chamber for quite a long time on opposition bench and have also voted on several occasions against the laws that are finally adopted and implemented, believe that these laws, when adopted in a legitimate way, are also mandatory for me to comply. This is despite the fact that I have had a disagreement during the procedure. The interpretation that I was critical of these legislations already at the time of their processing, and I can therefore, as if, withdraw from them, I certainly do not approve. At the same time, in understanding the sensitivity of this whole issue, and having actually seen in the past how one or the other step of the European Union's sanctioning nature has had a very negative impact on support for the Union, I look with great concern at the possible forthcoming legal dispute on the redistribution of refugees and quotas.
ParlaMint-EE_2017-06-14_U28	Thank you. Martin Helme, please!
ParlaMint-EE_2017-06-14_U29	Thank you. Fortunately, we have come a little closer to this question, which we had, whether Estonia is more in the position of defending the sovereignty of these countries or thinks that Brussels has the right to roll over the countries. Through the flowers I realized that we still have some sort of small desire to see sovereignty preserved. But I have a simple question. After all, this whole dispute is actually ruthlessly revealing how unvoluntary the whole quota system is. Estonia has always talked about how quotas are voluntary and all the so-called refugees we accept here have been voluntarily taken. However, those countries that did not voluntarily accept refugees are now being threatened with fines, under the heavy fists of the Commission. After all, it goes back to sovereignty. If some countries decide that the Commission has no right to send us people here, then so it is. And when the Commission starts pushing it through with force, we have the right to restore our sovereignty and leave the European Union. Don't you think that's what's gonna happen if the Commission continues this heavy fist tactic?
ParlaMint-EE_2017-06-14_U30	Thank you. If you answer strictly only the question at the end of your speech, then no. In my opinion, Estonia and the other 27 countries of the European Union are not currently facing such a radical step to decide to leave the European Union. I am optimistic about the future of the European Union, despite the fact that the next two years will certainly be difficult in the light of the Brexit negotiations. There is certainly a 'legal side' on this issue. As I have already said many times, I do not think that we ourselves can apply or comply with the binding legislation adopted in any way selectively, i.e. we cannot only comply with those that we like and not comply with those that we do not like. If there is a reasoned opinion that one or the other instrument is not binding, it is certainly possible to refer that opinion to the court for confirmation. In fact, this is a very sensible way of resolving a difference of opinion, because in such cases there are certainly worse ways of finding out the truth than a judicial dispute. Sovereignty is nothing to do with the Member States or with the European Union. Indeed, we have surrendered our sovereignty in a number of areas, due to the very complex contractual base of the European Union. We have not handed it over to some abstract third party, but have given the European Union joint decision-making powers on some issues, so we ourselves are, to a lesser or greater extent, codecision. As such, this issue of 'governance' must also be seen not only in the context of migration, but also more broadly. I think it is extremely reasonable to represent our interests in the world on a number of issues that are important to us, including security, using a much greater weight and visibility of the European Union.
ParlaMint-EE_2017-06-14_U31	I will conclude on this issue.

ParlaMint-EE_2017-06-14_U42	Foreign Minister Sven Mikser is asking Andres Herkel. Please!
ParlaMint-EE_2017-06-14_U43	Thank you, Mr. Chairman. Honourable Minister of Foreign Affairs! I would like to end this season with your help on such a completely political-intelligent issue, perhaps to ask where the European idea is going. Especially from this example, which is now the major election of Macron and his movement in France. The second round of parliamentary elections is still ahead, but everyone talks about traditional parties losing, especially the Social Democrats. So far, we have had the overwhelming interpretation of politics that confrontation is what may have a negative impact on society, but it brings votes in elections. Still, it's a right-wing and left-wing fight up very sharp. Now one man, one party in France, wins the election under the slogan 'Ni de droite, ni de gauche', i.e. neither right nor left. What conclusions do we draw from this, and what conclusions do the Social Democrats draw?
ParlaMint-EE_2017-06-14_U44	I certainly do not represent the positions of the Ministry of Foreign Affairs or the Government of the Republic of Estonia in response to this extraordinary philosophical question. It is clear that major changes are taking place in European society, some of which are due to long-term political alienation, some of which are reflected in increasing political polarisation, some certainly reflect a certain reaction to political polarisation and some antagonisation of political party views. We have seen such enthusiastic elections, where a very large part of the electorate can be mobilised, not on a 'traditional ideological axis', but around another idea. If we go back a little, then in 2008, for example, the Presidential elections in the United States and some time later, just a few years ago, for example, in the Canadian elections, even though it can be said that it was a traditional party that was able to mobilise public opinion around such an inspiring idea, and now, lastly, in the French elections. However, surprises in the elections to the large European countries are not limited to France. The recent parliamentary elections in Britain also probably gave a very surprising result to many who followed the electoral campaign, both inside and outside. To a certain extent, the search in society is also reflected by the fact that changes in technological and information technology, as well as changes in the labour market and the nature of work, have been very rapid. Not all levels of society and stakeholders have been able to accompany them. That is why we can certainly see such political shocks in the future in the conditions of democracy.
ParlaMint-EE_2017-06-14_U45	Andres Herkel, please!

It's understandable. At the same time, the solutions proposed by the Estonian Social Democrats speak of the need not to seek solutions from somewhere in the golden past, when the dominant industries were the heavy industry and the mining industry, but to focus their eyes on the future, where those economic areas that bring growth and create opportunities are located somewhere else and actually require a much greater contribution from society to human resources.

ParlaMint-EE_2017-06-14_U83	Aivar Sõerd, please!
ParlaMint-EE_2017-06-14_U84	Honourable Chairman! Ladies and gentlemen, On behalf of the Reform Party Group, I am handing over the draft law amending the Income Tax Act. The purpose of the draft law is to restore the possibility of submitting a joint income tax return for spouses in the Income Tax Act. This means that both spouses' incomes and deductions from incomes are added together. In Estonian tax law, the principle that spouses can submit a joint tax return has been in force all along the way. As I said, it basically means that all income and deductions are added in the joint tax return. The joint declaration also follows from the current family law and the logic of the common property of the spouses. Marriage usually means a contract of common property and obligations. This also means the right to a common tax return. Family policy is a value and a principle that should be important and high-ranking in politics. The Reform Party Group will re-submit a draft re-establishment of joint declarations because the Government Coalition has not fulfilled its promise and has not restored the possibility for spouses to submit a joint tax return. The coalition promised to restore it in words, but the joint declarations have not been restored in the draft amendment to the Income Tax Act, the VAT Act and the Traffic Act, as proposed by the Government, 458 which has been discussed today. The basic principle of the Joint Declaration, by which income and income deductions are added in the joint declaration of spouses, has not been restored. Even on the contrary, the Income Tax Act does not even have the same concept as the joint income tax return of spouses. For the Reform Party Group, valuing family policy is an important principle. In any case, a family-friendly legislative amendment has been initiated and will restore joint tax returns in full. Thank you for your attention.

Now, in order to implement this defence cooperation, it is necessary to make appropriate amendments to the VAT law and the National Defence Act, which we are currently dealing with in draft 431. I must say that, in international practice, countries conclude agreements on the status of armed forces between themselves, for example by sending members of their armed forces to another country for an operation or for other defence cooperation. Estonia has also concluded such agreements to ensure the highest possible legal status of its defence forces in foreign operations. For example, with Kuwait, we have made such an agreement in our time. The status agreements negotiated between NATO and the European Union have also been necessary for Estonia in several operations. In order to put this issue in an even wider context, the United States of America has entered into similar, so-called NATO SOFA-additional agreements with a number of other European countries with which the United States has close bilateral relations, such as Germany, the Netherlands, Bulgaria, Poland, Spain and, by now, Latvia and Lithuania, which ratified it in March and February respectively. Estonia has also concluded an additional agreement with Germany. I am speaking here on behalf of the Social Democratic Party and the Group. Certainly, we support the adoption of this draft. I do not think it is necessary to explain that the international dimension of our defence and the presence of our allies, especially, of course, the presence of the United States in our region, is crucial in the current security situation. Apart from strengthening our own defence capabilities, we have worked very hard to have our allies here. Consequently, we must obviously make their presence as smooth as possible with us, give it full legal clarity and, of course, make it as effective as possible. This bill undoubtedly supports all of this. This will increase the security of our region more widely and also narrowly our security in Estonia. ParlaMint-EE_2017-06-14_U97	Ants Laaneots on behalf of the Reform Party Group, please! Three minutes extra time.
ParlaMint-EE_2017-06-14_U98	Mr President, I would like to thank you very much. Ladies and gentlemen, I'll start with a brief look back on history. After the end of the Cold War in the 1990s and the break-up of the Soviet Union in December 1991, euphoria arose in both the West and Russia, that the era of great wars in Europe is now over and that all countries will live in friendship and cooperation. This was more or less the case in the last decade of the second millennium. Russia started a restructuring of society and a laborious transition from a socialist economic system to a market economy. The West tried to help him. Germany built residential homes for Russian troops returning home from the former forces beyond the borders of the Soviet Union. The Americans helped to deactivate the nuclear reactors of Soviet submarines, which have developed dozens of their resources and have been standing in the North Sea naval bases for years, and to defuse the vast supply of chemical weapons in the Soviet Union. NATO, in turn, had a serious debate in 1992 on whether not to liquidate the international defence organisation that is now becoming unnecessary. However, it was decided to leave NATO, but with rather limited tasks, which consisted mainly in the implementation of peacekeeping and peace-building tasks within the coalition forces. It no longer required large armies, and European radical disarmament began. Everything changed in 2000 with Vladimir Putin becoming President of Russia. The most important task for him is not the development of the country's economy and the improvement of the welfare of the people, but the expansion and the glory of the country. A book by the Kremlin, published in 2009, called 'New Russian Doctrine. The time to stretch out the wings' is the two strategic goals set for Russia: the closest to them, to restore control of the post-Soviet space, and the final, to become a global administrator, if necessary, by using force. The Assault on Georgia in 2008 and the annexation of Crimea and the invasion of Ukraine in 2014 confirm this. The feverish increase in the armed forces in the western Russian military community, which is adjacent to us, suggests that the order would have reached the Baltic States as well. With aggression against its neighbours, Putin has opposed himself to the whole democratic world. His actions have forced the Western countries to impose sanctions on Russia and to re-launch. This is also why the Eastern European countries of the Alliance feel threatened and have called for NATO troops to be deployed on their territory. This request was also accepted by the Alliance with its official decisions made last year in Warsaw. On the territory of Estonia are units of allies, for an indefinite period of time. They didn't come here for overeating or tour, but to ensure our security and, if necessary, to sacrifice themselves to protect our small country. Our task is to make the lives of men and women in units as comfortable as possible and to regulate their rights and obligations. These are the reasons why we passed a law last week called the Defence Cooperation Agreement between the Government of the Republic of Estonia and the Government of the United States of America and we are confirming today the second law on the implementation of the Defence Cooperation Agreement between the Republic of Estonia and the United States of America. This draft amends the VAT Act and the National Defence Act. The amendments to the VAT Act supplement the list of persons benefiting from exemption for the importation of goods for personal purposes and the refund of value added tax upon receipt of goods or services in Estonia, if provided for in the international agreement. According to the amendment to the National Defence Act, a member of the contract partner's staff who provides services to foreign armed forces can be granted an entry permit in Estonia on the same basis as a person related to foreign armed forces. The draft supports defence cooperation within NATO and, within the framework of the principle of collective self-defence, promotes the arrival and long-term stay of the armed forces units of the Allied States in Estonia. The amendments are planned to take effect from 1 July inclusive. Now for an unpleasant case. During the ratification of last week's agreement between the Republic of Estonia and the United States of America on defence cooperation, several members of the Prime Minister's party did not vote in favour. In fact, Mr. Stalnukhin and some of his associates walked out of the scene. This behaviour of the government's leading party is incomprehensible. While Mrs Kersti Sarapuu, Chairman of the Centre Party Group, explained that she did not vote on her wards because she was engaged in other duties, Mikhail Stalnukhin, a member of the Centre Party, stated by the media that she could not trust the US. It is difficult to understand what is happening in this party at all, especially in the area of security. This is complemented by tools for restoring the revenue base. So next year it will be 30 million, the next 45 million and the next 55 million in 2020. This is certainly important because it allows municipalities to become more substantive and powerful and to do things that are important to people. After all, the municipality is the administrative level closest to people. This increases the overall budget of municipalities from 23 to 24% to 28 to 29%, or just 5 to 6%. So it's actually a long way to go to get close to the Nordic countries. There is one important thing in the next draft law that the Free Party has emphasised here. This is to increase the possibilities for settlement units and villages to exercise the so-called right to self-determination or to decide which administrative unit they want to belong to. In fact, the limit on the majority of councils falls from two thirds to 51%. This is a very important change because it is much easier for councils to make decisions. It is clear that municipalities, which are protected by the Constitution and the European Charter for Local Government, cannot be deprived of such an important right. Finally, it is worth mentioning that the restoration of the revenue base, which is in the government and which will certainly reach us here, means that almost 50 million, which have been taken from the municipalities every year since 2009, will now come back. So this is important. I think we're going to take the first important step today, but I'm sure we have a long way to go. So here we can deal with matters of administrative reform and state reform in several sessions. I had a plan and a task to call on everyone, on behalf of the Centre Party, to vote in favour of these two drafts. I am pleased that I can simply welcome and note that the other groups also agree. But maybe the most important thing is to lower the tree age that has entered the bill. Any demagogic argument as to why this is necessary is not competent. Our position is that it should not be changed; it should remain the same. And we stress once again that we believe that this move is made primarily to gain greater profits from RMK, on the other hand because of pressure from industry, because it is the most high-quality timber that grows massively in the Estonian forest. The course of the procedure of this law and all the debates that have taken place in this connection have led to our idea so far that there is not really a social or, let's say, a national agreement in Estonia on the balance of forests: for example, how much tourism companies benefit, how much social benefits, how much cultural benefits etc. Also in economic terms, of all the debates that have followed this legislative amendment, it has become clear that there are very different views on forest growth and felling volumes. This means that we do not have a national agreement that you can see, if we cut more and younger than this, our forest will not recover. This is not the case, and we continue to call for scientists from the Estonian public and also from different perspectives to find this focus so that balance is maintained. I must certainly conclude by saying that the Free Party is not a total opponent of deforestation. I even live in a log house, and I don't know what wonderful things are made of wood. We understand very well that the timber industry is an important industry for Estonia. But on the other hand, we also realize that if we break this balance, we lose one of the highest values of the forest. Thus, as a forceful humiliation of the tree age has been introduced, the Free Party will vote against this law. Thank you.

ParlaMint-EE_2017-06-14_U152	On behalf of the Group of the Toomas Kivimägi Reform Party.
ParlaMint-EE_2017-06-14_U153	Mr President, thank you very much. Honourable Members of the Riigikogu! The Reform Party Group also does not support this draft, for the following reasons. First of all, this bill is presented, among other things, as taxation of transits through Estonia, i.e. vehicles registered abroad, but it is only a small part of the tax burden that actually happens to Estonian entrepreneurs. 20% is estimated to be the proportion that owners of vehicles registered abroad have to pay when driving on Estonian roads, and this is in the order of 3.4 million euros. But Estonian taxpayers, Estonian entrepreneurs, Estonian transport companies will have a total of 13.6 million. Perhaps 80% of the total additional income planned by this bill will actually be borne by Estonian entrepreneurs. Secondly, the Reform Party Group, first and foremost, does not consider it right, as the Free Party Group expressed here, to apply road user charges to trucks with a maximum mass exceeding 3.5 tonnes up to and including 12 tonnes. And once again, the reason is that this will certainly lead to an increase in prices for end-users, because the cars that carry food, construction materials and other essential goods that all of us, Estonians, need, also fit into this range. Of course, this cost will not be borne by transport companies, but will eventually be borne by taxpayers. They have to pay the cost when they go to the store to buy bread, milk, or building materials. However, we do in itself support the introduction of road user charges for trucks with a maximum weight of over 12 tonnes, which is also part of this draft. During the second reading, we proposed that this rate be somewhat lower than the one in the current draft, which is 16% lower in size. This proposal was primarily due to the fact that we would have similar conditions to neighbouring countries. And this fee for road use for trucks with a maximum weight of more than 12 tonnes was primarily based on the Latvian level. The second reason why we actually support the taxation of trucks with a maximum weight of over 12 tonnes is because of where I started. If the bill is adopted in the form that the coalition has put it on our table today, the owners of vehicles registered abroad will indeed only pay 20% of the total fee for road use and Estonia will bear 80%. But if we only took trucks with a maximum weight exceeding 12 tonnes, the proportion of vehicles registered abroad would undoubtedly be significantly higher than the proportion of vehicles registered in Estonia. I can give you an example that does not claim absolute truth. I drive at least twice a week between Tallinn and Pärnu. For one Estonian truck with a maximum weight of over 12 tonnes, I will meet six or seven trucks registered abroad on the road with a maximum weight of over 12 tonnes. Perhaps I would argue that if we tax only cars with a maximum weight of more than 12 tonnes, the proportion of vehicles registered abroad is actually higher than the draft currently proposes. One interesting nuance that has been repeatedly referred to is that the draft has been prepared in haste. It is also a sign of what the previous rapporteur said that one argument has been made that EU directives do not only allow trucks with a maximum mass of more than 12 tonnes to be taxed. This argument is not correct, as Denmark and Sweden have already received such an exception, which tax only trucks with a maximum weight of more than 12 tonnes with a road user charge. In fact, the Minister had the same desire, which he told us at first reading from this panel. But it really seems to me that since these revenues have already been taken into account in the next budget... I'm asking for three more minutes!

ParlaMint-EE_2017-06-14_U163	Jaanus Marrand on behalf of the Group of the Social Democratic Party.
ParlaMint-EE_2017-06-14_U164	Dear Vice-Chairman, Ladies and gentlemen, Today's final vote on the draft law amending the Public Transport Act, or the Uber Act, has been processed and baked for over a year. The draft has been raised not only by us here in Estonia, but by not exaggerating the whole world. This is a case of the complex problem of the collaborative economy, or the development of a new economy, which has been the subject of attention, let us remember, then, in Paris, New York, Berlin. On the one hand, such a great deal of attention highlights a certain vanity, the desire to be one of the first to be able to resolve all parties to the so-called Uber issue satisfactorily. The question is how to put one flagship of the old service economy, a taxi service together with a much different and more flexible sharing economy with a new approach to labour and working time. This vanity also meant that the initiators, or a group of members of the Riigikogu, initially tried to find very simple solutions. This situation is best described in the first version of the draft, which amended the Public Transport Act by adding a deal to it, and it was scrupulously argued that it was not public transport. We tried, figuratively speaking, to make it clear that he who jumps around in the garden with long ears and pussy tails and eats carrots is not a rabbit, even though he looks like this. As a natural reaction, it was followed by quite important petitions from the actual taxi service providers and let us admit that taximen were right. In the second stage, we arrived at a place where we dealt with it in a way that okay, let's put it aside, but the problem is that let's not talk about having the same kind of insurance as taxis or other cars. This, too, was a slightly simplified voluntaristic approach, but in the end we came to an agreement on this, too. On the final option, which is here before you, I say with full conviction that it is one of the better discussed drafts, where compromises have also been found across the Chamber of Riigikogu and also with all taxi service providers. The solution is pretty elegant. On the one hand, it liberalises the entire taxi market, on the other hand it introduces innovative elements, organises the taxi market. And the final result of the disputes was that we have room for electronic platforms, time providers using distance measurement, as well as traditional taxis with plafone and taxi meters. The new one can coexist with the old and traditional ones, which is actually the main key to this draft solution, which could serve as an example for other areas. Let us be honest, all of this is inevitably ahead of us in almost every area, because the world is changing faster than we can predict. Three days ago, I bet an innovation trust with a man who claimed that what you and Uber are messing with in seven years' time at the latest is 30% of the Estonian taxi market in self-driving cars and no drivers are needed at all. True, I was more skeptical. But to be honest, I wouldn't mind losing that bet, because progress is faster than we can foresee. In short, the changes are coming to. it's clear. A societal challenge is the increasing speed of change, which is becoming increasingly difficult to cope with. It's a little bit of a pass. We recall that by 1900, human knowledge doubled in about a century, but by the middle of the 20th century, it was every 25 years. The amount of information now doubles once a year, and the Internet of Things will lead to a doubling of global information every 12 hours in the coming years. There has never been such a thing in human history, and the world cannot handle it. Ten years ago, we listened to the radio in the car to find out about the jams ahead. We are now using an app that brings to us in real time the speed of mobile phones of other road users on every section of the road. In addition to everyday life, technological development and globalisation also affect the economy. The economy, in turn, affects people's work. The development of the digital economy loses millions of jobs in the world every year. In the near future, self-driving trucks will free nearly five million drivers in Europe, and this is a matter for the next decades, not for the century. Someone will be unemployed for technological reasons, but new jobs will be added to other areas. It is with these difficult challenges that the draft law on the amendment of public transport, on which we are voting today, is going to be put to the chest, in a rather primitive form at first. It is one of the first swallows to try to put together an old and new economy and can do it in a better way than it has done elsewhere in Europe. In short...

ParlaMint-EE_2017-06-14_U179	23rd item: Second reading of draft Act amending the Law on district heating initiated by the Government of the Republic. I would like to ask Jaanus Marrand, member of the Economic Commission, to present the chair.
ParlaMint-EE_2017-06-14_U180	Mr President, I would like to thank you very much. Ladies and gentlemen, The draft Act amending the Law on district heating 264 before you was initiated by the Government of the Republic on 13 June last year. The first reading took place on 9 November and by the deadline for submitting amendments, no amendments to the draft were received from the Riigikogu by 22 February. During the course of the procedure, the Estonian Power Stations and District heating Association, Eesti Energia AS and the non-profit organisation Estonian Turbali, who proposed amendments to the draft. The representative of the initiator of the draft act forwarded his views on the proposals received to the Commission. The Commission also received a letter from the Competition Authority concerning these proposals. The committee asked the Legal and Analytical Department of the Chancellery of the Riigikogu for an opinion on the amendments. The Ministry of Economic Affairs and Communications also received some proposals in the course of their work. In addition, the initiator's representative formulated several proposals as a result of discussions in the working group. The preparation of amendments for discussion in the lead committee took place on 6 April and 13 April, including in Commission working groups involving representatives of the initiator. The text of the draft was editorially edited between the two readings. During the period between first and second reading, the Committee on Economic Affairs discussed the draft at its three meetings. Firstly, on 30 May, there was a debate on the amendments and a hearing on the views of the citizens who referred to the Commission. Timo Tatar, Head of the Energy Department of the Ministry of Economic Affairs and Communications and Rein Vaks, who presented the content of the amendments, were invited to the meeting of the Commission as representatives of the initiator of the draft. A longer debate was held on Amendment 5 on the reference price. The main thrust of its report is mainly on this proposal for a reference price, as other proposals are relatively editorial. This fifth proposal is perhaps the most fundamental and was discussed in the Riigikogu. A total of 13 proposals were drawn up in the Committee on Economic Affairs of the Riigikogu, but, as I said, most of them are editorial. I will now introduce a bit of the debate on the reference price in which the members of the Commission expressed their views. Chairman of the Commission Aivar Kokk stated that the Competition Authority should always retain the right to verify whether the district heating company is making an excessive profit. There was also doubts about the proposed design, according to which the Minister would endorse the principles on the basis of which the Competition Authority would propose a specific reference price. It was thought that this might not be compatible with the Constitution. It was asked whether there was a risk that the Competition Authority would raise the reference price very high in order to reduce its workload. The representatives of the Ministry confirmed that the Competition Authority would not have the freedom to set the price as they wish, but should be guided by the principles established by the Ministerial Regulation. The question also arose as to whether in Ida-Virumaa, when adopting the reference price regulation, there would be a exponential increase in the price of district heating. Ida-Virumaa is known to be a special place, especially in Narva, where the by-product of electricity production is warm. So it is mainly waste heat, which is why energy prices are quite low. The representatives of the Ministry explained that according to competition law, no company has the right to abuse its dominant position on the market. With the addition of heat pumps, solar panels and other heating products, the heating market opens up to competition, which should keep the price down. Otherwise, if the price is too high, heat consumers simply leave the district heating network and look for alternative options. As a result, the district heating company would lose even more. The representatives of the Ministry once again confirmed that the risk was grounded by the competition law, which provides for strong sanctions in the event of a breach of competition rules, and explained that, in the event of a price change, the consumer could contact the Competition Authority to determine whether the company had taken advantage of the dominant position in the market by increasing the price. Most of the time, there is no basis for a sharp rise in prices. There was also a debate that the ex post follow-up procedure would be a time-consuming exercise and whether such risk-taking would make sense. The representatives of the Ministry pointed out that several bidders are still selling under the price limit set by the Competition Authority. The district heating sector is, rather, moving towards closer market competition. The numerical price increase rate was excluded from the regulation of the reference price because it was considered that this provision would have encouraged price increases. Supervision under competition law is sufficient, so there is no need to set numerical limits on the rate of increase in the draft. Whether the tenderer sells below the price limit or the reference price, it is legally obliged to comply with the principle of the design of the cost components. It was clarified that, after the amendment of the law, the providers of district heating have no grounds for sudden price increases. In short, the district heating law was mainly under discussion, I repeat, the subject of the reference price. Finally, in the debate, it came to the conclusion that one exception could be introduced, which concerns above all, as I mentioned earlier, Narva, where Estonia has a completely exceptional situation, so-called residual heat. This is also stated in the fifth amendment, which reflects the reference price. To conclude, there are 290 district heating regions in Estonia and in most of them prices are far above the so-called reference price. Only 25 district heating regions are priced below the hypothetical reference price. The level of the reference price was initially estimated at close to EUR 50 per megawatt-hour. To be clear, the reference price could be an engine or a'screw' that would force companies to invest. In particular, those companies that are slightly above this reference price to invest and achieve a situation where they do not have to coordinate each of their price movements with the Competition Authority. At its first meeting on 30 May, all amendments were examined and procedural decisions were taken. It was decided to put the draft on the plenary agenda by 7 June, to propose that the second reading be closed and that the draft be sent to the third reading today. In fact, the Commission met again on 5 June. A number of questions had still been raised in relation to the same reference price and it was considered that this whole issue should still be discussed in depth in the working group, even with regard to the same issue of constitutionality and certain exceptions. It was therefore decided that it would still be unreasonable to put the draft on the agenda by 7 June. So on 5 June it was decided that this draft could be removed from the agenda and brought to this second reading by today, 14 June. There was also a suggestion that it would be reasonable to stop reading the second draft. We now come to the meeting of the Commission on 12 June, where, after these changes to the reference price regulation, Timo Tatar, Head of the Energy Department of the Ministry of Economic Affairs and Communications, and Rein Vaks, expert, again explained the content of the amendments and answered further questions. Certain changes were introduced as a result of the recommendation of the Legal and Analytical Department of the Riigikogu. They assessed the alternative proposed in the past, according to which a reference price would be set by the Competition Authority, and thought that this would most likely be unconstitutional. It was therefore proposed that this price be set by the Minister for Economic Affairs and Infrastructure. There were a number of other debates. Finally, on the proposal of the Minister for Economic Affairs and Infrastructure, the Commission came to the conclusion that the subject still needs to be addressed, analysed and that it would be reasonable to interrupt the second reading. The Committee on Economic Affairs proposed that this draft be put to second reading by today's date, that the second reading be suspended and that the deadline for amendments be set at 17 p.m. on 18 September. Thank you for your attention.

So, a week ago, there was a conference where I was solemnly informed that someone had already been able to calculate this reference price and that it was EUR 50. So this situation arises, for example, in my home town of Narva, the tariff is 35,62. If you read the law, everything in it, then the district heating service provider will have the opportunity to raise this tariff from 35,62 to 50 under the reference price. And all he has to do after that is report it to the Competition Authority. And the Competition Authority does not have to check that the price increase complied with the law or did not comply with the law. I admit our conversation wasn't very polite. But it was a total shock for me to know that such a law was being prepared. During the past period, our people paid 12 million dollars for the heat, and the city government paid 1.3 million dollars out of the city budget. I'm rounding, of course. There is now a chance that the burden on people will increase by 5.5 million and that the burden on the city administration will increase by 40% of this amount. Hey, how does a thing like that get in here anyway? However, a comforting amendment is being made, which is why there should be peace in the house. This is the first paragraph of § 9 (1) of the draft law, where this reference price is still written as follows: '... where the heat produced in the grid area is not produced with a cogeneration unit of heat and electricity with a rated electrical output exceeding 200 MW. ' Great, it's like taking Narva out of the way. But how am I supposed to feel about this? After all, in Narva, perhaps this is a surprise for you, the price of heat is calculated not on the basis of what is actually done there, but on the basis of gas. At the time, there were basic disputed methodologies on how to calculate the price of electricity and heat for cogeneration, how to distribute them, three: physical, economic, etc. And in total there are 30 models that are bonded one way or the other. Since this got complicated and the city of Narva came to me with the then Energy Market Inspectorate for five years in court, the solution was, after all, that we calculate the warm price on the basis of gas. I mean, we're going to have a huge investment in gas boiler houses built in power plants, right, if the temperature goes below minus 18 degrees, if memory doesn't fool me. You don't need it until then, but we're going to calculate all that volume. The easiest way to calculate the so-called "reference price" is if no agreement can be reached. And so, at first, Narva's going to get the reference price down. In a few years it will be reviewed and then the reference price will be not 50, but 70. There are dozens of district heating regions where prices can be freely increased. How does it work? I don't understand it. And another thing. I'm always asked, "Hey, Chairman of the Monetary Affairs Committee, whether there's been a meeting with the interest groups, if it's been done." The Chairman of the Riigikogu tells me this all the time. And we're trying. If I sum up, we've met some 30 organisations under these last five laws that we've worked with anyway. But it's basically missing. This is how you have happened, that those who sell, but those who buy, were involved in the development of this legislative amendment. All municipalities with district heating areas depend on this law. As far as I am concerned, you have not invited anyone to attend or talked to anyone at the time of development or here in the Riigikogu. How is it even possible that one of the parties who is going to sell is going to write the law for those who are going to buy it later? In my opinion, of course, it would be best to recall this draft, to expel it from the procedure and to start the process again when it is necessary. But if you stop reading, you should in any case make sure that one hundred percent of this autumn is also attended by local authorities. Thank you.
ParlaMint-EE_2017-06-14_U191	Jaanus Marrand, please!
ParlaMint-EE_2017-06-14_U192	Mr President, I would like to thank you very much. Ladies and gentlemen, I came here to argue with Mikhail's views a little bit. First of all, the coordination of this draft or the discussions with stakeholders. As I said here in the Commission's report, this draft has actually been processed for three or four years. Already during the previous composition of the Riigikogu, this bill, including the same reference price, was made quite far. And it was then very clearly coordinated with local authorities and all stakeholders. Perhaps in this four or five years, this has been very, very much coordinated and discussed with stakeholders. Why is this reference price here? It may be very difficult to understand, but there is one very clear principle why this is here and why this is being done. During these long discussions, I was able to learn this for myself. In fact, it is a question of the whole district heating system. After all, the volume of district heating is decreasing all the time and it is decreasing first and foremost because of any alternative heating option. It is natural that they are used in all kinds of heat pumps, solar energy, etc. This bill also states that in these cases it is possible to leave the district heating network. However, there is a need to find some kind of incentive for district heating to function as effectively as possible in its steadily decreasing volumes. That is the idea that the same reference price issue has been introduced here. Mikhail pointed out that what this is in the Estonian circumstances, this economically efficient modeled district heating system price, the price of heat in the network area. To find out, the Competition Authority has a very large information base. And the Tallinn University of Technology has actually developed for us a model that takes into account existing technology, existing climate, existing routes in Estonia. So in that sense, you might think that the price is still the same. As regards whether, immediately, the warm price will be raised to this maximum possible level, I think this is a question of how the functioning of the market economy will be understood at all. In reality, as I said, there are 270.280 network areas in Estonia and only 25 of them are currently able to offer a price below this hypothetical EUR 50 per megawatt-hour. Most people have a warm price at 50, 60, 70 or 80 euros. However, none of those below the EUR 50 threshold (including Narva) are asking for the principle of competition after the price they have been charged at the marginal price. Nor does Narva, where the actual price is EUR 35 per megawatt-hour, although the marginal price is EUR 37. So you could always ask for more, but you don't ask for that maximum. The same is true in Tallinn and in many other places. So, in fact, this reference price would give'screw', above all, to those who have a warm price of 55 or 60 euros per megawatt-hour. They would know that if they were able to invest, they would achieve a better price level and be able to handle less red tape. They need such certainty about the future. It would therefore be quite reasonable to introduce this reference price here. As I said, in reality there are quite a few who would escape this regulation. And in the case of Narva, it is possible to establish a distinction which takes into account precisely the fact that there is residual heat, not the need to produce heat specifically.

Therefore, I am not very optimistic and do not believe that by the end of the first week of September we will have nice and constructive amendments on the table. Thank you.

ParlaMint-EE_2017-06-14_U199	Second reading of the draft Act amending the Insurance Pensions Act and the Investment Funds Act initiated by the Government of the Republic. The Commission's rapporteur is Mikhail Stalnukhin, Chairman of the Monetary Commission. Please!
ParlaMint-EE_2017-06-14_U200	Mr. Chairman! Ladies and gentlemen, This draft law was in the large hall at first reading on 4 May. The deadline for amendments was 18 May. No amendments were received from the members of the Riigikogu, committees or political groups by that date. However, the Commission prepared three amendments in cooperation with the Ministry of Finance. Number one. It is here that the mandate given to the Minister of Finance to establish the forms of statements is repealed. The Act confers these powers on the Central Register of Securities. By mistake, however, one of the places, in particular § 31 (3) of the Law, had retained the ministerial mandate and had to be removed from it. So this is a technical amendment. Number two. This has been done on the basis of point 1 of their letter of 17 May, on the proposal of the Estonian Insurance Association. As it was supported by the initiator of the draft proposal, it was also drafted as an amendment. Here is an explanation that § 49 is supplemented by paragraph 12 and states that the withdrawal from the pension contract is not subject to the third sentence of § 433 (1) of the Law of Obligations Act or to § 56 of the Law of Obligations Act. The Savings Pensions Act provides for a distinction in the award of the contract compared to the Law of Obligations Act. According to this, the customer has the right to withdraw from a distance contract within 14 days of its conclusion. Upon expiry of the deadline, the registrar shall arrange for the redemption of pension fund units and the inclusion of funds by the insurance undertaking. In other similar situations, where the law on funded pensions provides for differences in relation to the Law of Obligations Act, the draft contains provisions which clearly exclude the application of the relevant regulation of the Law of Obligations Act. Amendment 3 Paragraph 1 (48) is deleted from the draft, which wished to supplement the transitional provisions of the Pensions Act. The date of entry into force is now 21 May 2018 and time is quite enough, so this transitional provision is not needed. Before I inform you of the procedural decisions, it is also necessary to inform you why we discussed all this four times in committee during our sittings, when everything went so lightly and beautifully. This was due to the fact that the Commission, as once and as the court, sent letters of engagement to six organisations, received replies from three organisations, and one of them, specifically the Co-operative Future, made very interesting proposals. The proposal was to reduce the mandatory national pension rate of the pension contract (hereinafter RPM) by 700 times, which is EUR 123160, to 200 times, which is about EUR 35200; to allow all people who retire before 2025 to use the pension fund, regardless of how many savings they have accumulated in the second pillar account; and to supplement the funded pension law with an analysis obligation of the Ministry of Finance. The initiator accepted this proposal and even prepared a very substantial amendment, the main directions of which were as follows. It was suggested that an amount between EUR 50 and 200 RPM, or EUR 8800 and EUR 35200, would be subject to a life-long pension contract, with several pension contracts for the amount of 200 RPM. Point 2: In the case of an amount between 200 and 700 RPM, i.e. I will now round up between 35000 and 123000, the pension could be allowed to be paid on a fixed-term basis. The deadline could be set on the basis of an average life expectancy corresponding to the pensioner's age plus two years. In the case of an amount exceeding 700 RPM, the payment of money could be allowed at a time. We also approached the Committee on Social Affairs with this proposal and received, I would say, support from it. But then there was the feeling that such a great deal could not be done on the knee, and this was the Commission's consensual decision. In fact, this same future association of insurance companies mentioned also attended these meetings. They, too, felt that the Ministry of Finance should be given the task of doing an analysis of the impact of such an amendment and then come up with it in the autumn. Basically, this is quite a long time, so it would make sense for us to deal with it calmly. Thus, this amendment disappeared the most extensive and one that brings a great deal of new addition to the table of amendments. In the autumn, as we have already talked to the Committee on Social Affairs, we will move on to this issue, but already in a considered way. After that, a procedural decision, which was not original, had to be proposed to the Bureau to send the draft to the second reading of the large hall today, by 14 June, with a proposal to finish the second reading. This was proposed in a consensual manner. Thank you.

ParlaMint-EE_2017-06-14_U217	Second reading of draft Act 454 on simplified taxation of business income initiated by the Government of the Republic. The Commission's rapporteur is Mikhail Stalnukhin, Chairman of the Monetary Commission. Please!
ParlaMint-EE_2017-06-14_U218	Mr. Chairman! Ladies and gentlemen, The first reading of this draft law took place on 17 May. The deadline for amendments was set at 5.15 p.m. on 31 May. No amendments were received from committees, political groups or members of the Riigikogu by that day and by that time. However, one amendment had to be tabled on behalf of the Committee on Monetary Affairs. Paragraph 17 (1) of the draft is amended as it is necessary to amend Paragraph 6 (5) (3) of the Labour Market Services and Benefits Act. This provision lists the grounds on which a person is not counted as unemployed. The purpose of the amendment is only to supplement this list and to exclude registration as unemployed when a person provides a service or sells goods within the meaning of the simplified tax law on business income. Under the current Labour Market Services and Benefits Act, a person is not registered as unemployed even if he or she is employed in the public service. It was mistakenly out of the bill. The amendment therefore complements § 17 (1) of the draft with the words 'or is in the public service'. That's it. There was a committee meeting where we prepared the draft for a second reading. There were two letters. There were five interested organisations to whom we had sent letters of engagement. Two answers were given: Estonian Taxpayers' Association and the Banking Association. These questions, which they raised, were also submitted to the representatives of the government and received an answer. The main content of the letter of the Estonian Taxpayers' Association was that they consider that there are too many possibilities for tax optimisation in the draft and that, in essence, wage work can be taxed at half the price of the standard rate. The bank association was primarily interested in technical moments: how the tax is transferred to the Estonian Tax and Customs Board and how processes, tax reservations on the business account and the seizure of the business account are foreseen. Procedural decisions were taken at the meeting on 5 June. First, send the draft to second reading by 14 June, today. A decision to terminate the second reading was also taken by consensus. Thank you.

ParlaMint-EE_2017-06-14_U228	Second reading of Bill 457 of the Act on the Tax on Sweet Drinks, initiated by the Government of the Republic. Chairman of the Finance Commission, Mikhail Stalnukhin, please!
ParlaMint-EE_2017-06-14_U229	Mr. Chairman! Ladies and gentlemen, If you will allow me, I will first say that, at the committee meeting, I spoke about this previous matter, that when it starts to start, social advertising needs to be used. Without such a campaign, people won't take that into account. But now for the next item. The first reading of the draft decision took place on 17 May. By 31 May, the Group of the Estonian Reform Party tabled 11 amendments and Aivar Sõerd, member of the Riigikogu, tabled one amendment. We sent letters of engagement to 12 organisations and an interested group, and we received nine responses. Now, for the sake of the verbatim report, I will give a slightly more detailed overview of what took place in the Commission in the context of the debate on this draft. The Estonian Taxpayers' Association stated in its letter that the concern is the ease with which "discovered" new tax objects aimed at finding additional sources of income. The proposal not to adopt the draft as proposed has been made by the Association of Drinks Producers, the Estonian Food Industry Association and seven other organisations. As a major drawback of the draft, stakeholders have pointed out that the sweetened beverage tax does not meet health protection objectives. Predictably, the explanatory memorandum is accompanied by an analysis by the Ministry of Social Affairs, which claims otherwise. Stakeholders have also pointed out that the draft is contrary to the principle of good lawmaking and to the norm rules, as it was not preceded by a programme of development and an analysis of impacts is incomplete. Doubt has been expressed as to whether the draft is compatible with the Constitution of the Republic of Estonia and with European Union law. With regard to the implementation of the draft, doubts have been expressed as to whether the information systems will be completed by 1 January 2018. The immediate negative impact on businesses has been highlighted by the increase in administrative burden due to the production and submission of a monthly tax return. This is compounded by the fact that the draft has not been accompanied by the form of the desiccated beverage tax declaration introduced by the Ministerial Decree, which makes it impossible for economic operators to assess the administrative burden involved in the draft. The Monetary Commission met stakeholders on 6 June. This session was attended by the Estonian Food Industry Association, the Estonian Merchant Men's Association, the Association of Soft Drinks Producers, the Estonian Chamber of Commerce and Industry and Tallink Duty Free AS. Representatives of the Estonian Association of Pharmacists and the Estonian Association of Medicinal Products Wholesalers announced that they do not wish to participate in the debate. They confirmed that, in cooperation with the Ministry of Finance, they have found a solution to their problem, and it is now possible to confirm that the new tax on Glucomedics and other such beverages used to test sugar tolerance is not applicable. The Estonian Taxpayers' Association and the Estonian Trade Union Confederation did not announce their wish to participate. During the sitting I am talking about, the participants gave a brief overview of their main points. The views of the representatives of the ministries on the views expressed were then heard. Representatives of the Ministry of Finance, the Ministry of Social Affairs and the Ministry of Rural Affairs participated. The representative of the initiator of the draft act explained that compliance with the Constitution was analysed in point 3.2 of the draft explanatory memorandum. The initiator of the draft is of the opinion that the draft is not in conflict with the Constitution here; it is not an unacceptable violation of the freedom to conduct a business. As regards the compatibility of the draft act with European Union law, the initiator's representative stated that it is difficult to assume a contradiction, as such a tax has also been imposed in other Member States, not all but quite a few. However, it was considered necessary to take into account the comment made by the Association of Drinks of Soft Drinks that the tax base should be as wide as possible, that market participants should be treated equally and that the tax base should certainly include drink yogurt, kefir and similar products. This change leads to the need to supplement Estonia's application for state aid with dairy products. We talked about the subject of the application for state aid before the first reading. At the moment, I can announce that the text will be completed on 16 June, which is Friday, as promised by the Ministry of Finance. Where State aid is granted, these products shall not be subject to tax. Such a solution was chosen in order to rule out unambiguously the dispute with the European Commission, which could arise because some products are subject to this tax and others are not. Representatives of the Ministry of Social Affairs gave an overview of the health protection objectives of the tax. Their presentation is included in the explanatory statement, which I have already mentioned. With regard to the additional administrative burden on businesses, it was acknowledged that any new tax would inevitably lead to an increase in administrative burden. In order to make it more predictable, the explanatory memorandum was authorised to include the draft implementing act, which is included in it. At the meeting with stakeholders, discussions were also held on the taxation of stocks (Article 12 of the draft new version). The initiator of the draft act explained that, according to the draft, the payer is the manufacturer, importer and customer of the sweetened drink from another Member State of the European Union when making it available for the first time in Estonia. From these persons, the sweetened drink usually moves out of Estonia, to the Estonian warehouse or directly to the place of sale. During the transition period, the obligation also arises to tax the sweetened drinks in the intermediate store. In this way, it is avoided that large quantities of sweetened beverages are stored in these warehouses, which should not be taxed. Okay. The initiators also said that, as of 1 January next year, not all sweetened drinks that are exchanged are automatically taxed, but that taxation takes place when the first event giving rise to taxation takes place. All drinks delivered to the point of sale before 1 January 2018 are exempt. The representative of Tallink Duty Free AS explained at the hearing how the sweetened beverage tax affects the shipping industry and proposed to complete the draft by introducing an exemption for the in-flight supplies carried on board water and aircraft. The proposal was supported by the Committee on Finance and presented as Amendment 15 on the list of amendments. Now I am talking about amendments. Amendments 1, 4, 9, 11, 14, 16, 17, 20, 21, 23 and 24 have been tabled by the Group of the Estonian Reform Party. The aim of these proposals is to remove all the paragraphs from the draft one by one because, as explained, the Group of the Estonian Reform Party does not support the introduction of a sweetened beverage tax. Okay. The author's explanation can be found in the list of amendments to Amendment 1. Staff announced that, according to the working arrangements, they should merge these amendments into one. In order not to do so, a Commission decision was needed. The Commission decided not to merge them and to keep these amendments on the list as separate amendments. Amendments 2, 3, 5, 6, 7, 8, 12, 13, 15, 18 and 19 have been prepared in the Committee on Monetary Affairs in cooperation with the initiator. Explanations of these are given in the list of amendments after each proposal. Amendment 10 has been drafted in the light of the proposal made by the Ministry of Social Affairs. This clarifies the taxation of a beverage sweetened with a sweetener. The aim is to motivate the sugar content of products to be reduced without the addition of sweeteners. The explanation is given in the list of amendments to the proposal. Amendment 22 was tabled by Mr Aivar Sõerd, member of the Riigikogu. Its purpose is to provide the economic operator with additional information on the implementing act and the electronic format of the declaration. The proposal requires that the information system necessary for the administration of the tax be completed at least three months before the payment obligation arises. On a proposal from the Monetary Commission, the Ministry of Finance supplemented the draft and as a result the powers conferred on the Minister were clarified. This is mentioned in Amendments 13 and 18. A draft implementing act was also submitted and annexed to the explanatory memorandum (Annex 1). This draft indicates the data to be included in the electronic declaration form. The Commission decided to propose that this draft be sent to the second reading in the Chamber today, 14 June, with a proposal to conclude the second reading. Of course, there was no consensus. Somewhere around here are the results of the vote, I'm looking for them in case anyone wants to know the exact result. I think six were in favour and five against, more or less. Thank you.

In fact, producers have already reduced sugar content by more than 12% over the last 17 years. Voluntaryly, a further 10% reduction in sugar by 2020 has been set. So to change people's healthy behaviour, something more than a new tax is needed, and the purpose of this tax is only to fill a budget hole. In order to meet health objectives, it is necessary to improve people's nutrition awareness and encourage people to eat and move healthyly. The labelling of products also needs to be improved.

ParlaMint-EE_2017-06-14_U364	After these speeches, it is difficult to add anything, but I still have something to say. The authors of the idea of the Sugar Tax have advocated that the introduction of a new tax will have an impact on people's health behaviour by directing producers to reduce the amount of sugar in sweetened beverages and by reducing the consumption of these drinks by buyers. The real aim, as we know, and as we can read from the previous debate, is instead to collect money from people in order to fulfil the expensive promises in the coalition agreement. Under the noble sign of health promotion, the country is being fattened up again, which is the real purpose of this draft. Even with the best of will, it is not possible to seriously talk about the positive effects of the sugar tax on human health if there is no analysis of this in the draft. Neither the draft nor the explanatory memorandum refers to any analysis or study linking, for example, the harmfulness and overweight of sweetened beverages. Moreover, according to the National Institute for Health Development, sweetened drinks account for only 1.5% of the daily consumption of Estonians. Sweet drinks are the least important sweet products, accounting for only 8% of the choice of this group, being long below bakery products, sweetened dairy products and, for example, candy chocolates, which have a significant impact on human health more than sweet drinks. However, the draft does not touch on solid sweets. Sugar is sugar, and excessive consumption is harmful in any case. When I asked Sven Sester, the customs Minister of Finance in the Riigikogu, why is it that, in liquid form, for example, Isostar's sports drink will cost more than powder in the future, he could not answer me. When I mix the powder with water at home, I get the same drink as when I buy liquid at the store. Now the coalition has improved itself, and the powders have been included in this bill, but this initial attitude has shown that, in fact, the priority is not changing people's health behaviour, but still money, money, money. In the light of the experience of other countries, people will start to consume more alternative, non-taxed products in the event of additional taxation of a particular product group, increasing border and smuggling and, of course, adding to the cost of tax administration. The introduction of additional and unexpected taxes reduces the attractiveness of the economic environment in the eyes of investors, both local and foreign. The emergence of these effects has been thoroughly demonstrated in a study commissioned by the European Commission. However, the government has not prepared any analyses to measure the economic impact of the sugar tax, as I have already said. The main thing is that none of the countries where the sweet tax has been applied has seen a positive impact on people's weight. In 2014, Denmark decided to waive the inefficient sweet tax from the 1930s for these reasons. Since there is still no European Commission authorisation to exempt natural juices, for example, health products are also becoming more expensive, yet this should not be an objective. It is very likely that the European Commission will not allow this exception at all, as we can read from today's debate. Finland also applied for a similar derogation to cookies in addition to chocolate, but they did not get it. Why should we assume or seemingly believe that we will be allowed to do so? This proves once again that the government is hoping to do something, but it turns out quite the opposite. The proposed tax amendment also has serious legal shortcomings, which have already been highlighted. The sweet tax constitutes an extremely disproportionate violation of fundamental rights as it violates producers' freedom to conduct business and the right to equal treatment. Moreover, good legislative practice has been ignored in the urgent procedure of the draft law. The government has justified this by the urgency of the law. However, what is so urgent is not stated in the explanatory memorandum. The proposed tax also runs counter to EU law as other products with high sugar content are not expected to be taxed. It would therefore be prohibited to grant State aid if the tax were introduced. If public health were actually to be improved, taxes would not be mentioned, but real steps would be taken to improve people's health behaviour. In my opinion, information and, above all, home example should be started, explaining to the child the importance of regular healthy eating, active exercise and adequate rest. There is no more important than a parent's role as an example. Next thing you know, it's important school. In the institutions of education, I think we could clean up unhealthy drinks and meals from the buffet. If the school is a parenting institution, let it be a healthy diet. Unfortunately, the average youth is becoming more and more physically passive. In my opinion, therefore, the number of hours of physical education should be increased to at least three hours a week in order to give young people real motivation to move more. The broader concept of mobility education proposed by the Ministry of Education and Research, which provides for some lessons to take place when moving in nature, is a step in the right direction if done right. Surely, though, this should not mean that already few physical education classes will be replaced by hiking in the forest in the context of other substances. I believe that mobility education, or integration of movement with other subjects such as biology, science or physics, must only be a supporting activity, apart from physical education. The exercise course must certainly not replace the physical education class. Of course, promoting public health should not be limited to activities at school and at home. Adults also need to be told what benefits come from a healthy lifestyle and how important it is to be healthy. Let's talk about things the way they are. A sugar levy in this form is necessary in particular to enable the State to cover its planned costs. People with taxes are not treated, there are other methods. These methods need to be given much greater emphasis by the government in the future if the dreams of better health of people in the draft explanatory memorandum are to be actually realised. Thank you.

ParlaMint-EE_2017-06-14_U399	Today's next item is the second reading of the draft law amending the Income Tax Act, the VAT Act and the Traffic Act initiated by the Government of the Republic.
