ParlaMint-EE_2017-06-14_U400	Mr President! Ladies and gentlemen, [[The manager's ringing the bell.]] On the previous agenda, there was talk of a mysterious half-year, during which we need to familiarise ourselves with the questions. I would like to begin by stating that this draft was launched on 4 May and was accepted on 8 May. It's a month. [[The manager's ringing the bell.]]
ParlaMint-EE_2017-06-14_U401	Ladies and gentlemen, Sorry, waitress! Unfortunately, the noise in the hall is so loud that the waitress is not heard. I ask my colleagues to have their talks outside the hall or to give them up! Please, waitress!
ParlaMint-EE_2017-06-14_U402	The first reading took place on 17 May and the deadline for submitting amendments was 5.15 p.m. on 31 May. After that, there were five other Commission meetings on the agenda. 13 amendments were received by the deadline for submitting amendments: three amendments were tabled by the Group of the Estonian Free Party and ten amendments were tabled by the Group of the Estonian Reform Party. The Committee on Finance discussed the draft five times after its first reading. The Commission, of course, asked stakeholders for opinions on the draft. They were presented by the Estonian Chamber of Commerce and Industry, the Estonian Employers' Confederation, the Estonian Taxpayers' Association, the Estonian Banking Association and the non-profit association that brings together Estonian members of the International Tax Law Association. The Committee on Finance agreed a meeting with the interest groups on 8 June, but you will remember that on 8 June there was suddenly a motion of distrust and an extension of the session. If we had known that only one question would be asked to the Minister, not 90, as has been the case during the debate on censures, then we would not have informed the interest groups that the Commission meeting would not be held. But that's what happened. Since there was no desire to keep people so respectable in the corridors for hours, we canceled this meeting. The following week, we took turns proposing Monday, Tuesday, Wednesday and Thursday, but none of these days were possible for interest groups to attend the meeting. However, the stakeholders submitted their proposals and the Commission examined the issues raised together with the specialists of the Ministry of Finance. Stakeholders' calls concerned the quality of tax legislation, the introduction of a so-called 'patent income tax', the advance income tax on credit institutions, the price of special incentives for passenger cars, the registration of a traffic register if the passenger car is used solely for the purposes of the work, and the employer's expenditure on the transport and accommodation of workers. The most numerous questions were raised and complaints were made about a pledge tax, which was proposed by the Ministry of Finance to be excluded from the draft. I must remind you that during this period there was an exchange of leadership between a coalition party and the coalition agreement went back to review. So we put this draft on the agenda, a couple of times the specialists from the Ministry of Finance came and gave us an opinion on one question or another, but we did not have any idea how the coalition agreement would be amended. However, in the end, the proposals resulting from the amendment of the coalition agreement came. That's when they came in with the Ministry of Finance, and there's eight of them. Amendment 1 allows spouses to make certain deductions jointly for tax purposes. Amendment 2 introduces a technical correction in the text of the law due to the loss of the allowance for low-wage earners. Amendment 4 is technical, specifying the location of the provision to be included in the law. Amendment 5 allows the employer to carry workers over 50 km away from the place of residence and the place of work on a vehicle with at least eight seats alongside the bus, without a special incentive charge. This amendment was proposed by the Chamber of Commerce. Amendment 7 deletes the introduction of a so-called 'patent income tax' in the draft text, which was the desire of most stakeholders, instead laying down a mechanism for the identification and definition of hidden profit allocations. This takes into account the proposals made by the Chamber of Commerce and the Banking Association. As a result, part of the amendment tabled by the Estonian Free Party Group has been taken into account, in memory it was No 10, and amendments 8, 9, 11 and 12 submitted separately by the Estonian Reform Party Group. Amendment 19 is technical and provides wordingual clarification. Amendment 20 states that when the vehicle database states that the company's vehicle is used exclusively for business travel, the state fee is not paid. This was proposed by the Estonian Taxpayers' Association. Amendment 21 clarifies the date of entry into force of some of the provisions. This will be done to ensure that the President has sufficient time to take his decision, but the benefits will apply retroactively from 1 July. The Commission did not consider it necessary to support Amendments 3 and 13 to 18 aimed at excluding the regulation of advances to credit institutions from the draft. The majority of the Commission supported the draft on this issue as initiated. Similarly, the Commission did not consider it justified to significantly reduce the price of the special benefit of using a work car for private purposes (amendment 6) since the average price of the special benefit calculated by the rates proposed in the draft is already below the average value of the benefit as estimated by the specialists of the Ministry of Finance. Technical and linguistic clarifications have also been made in the text of the draft act. The Commission's proposal was to send a draft law to the Chamber today, 14 June and to conclude the second reading. Thank you.

Changes to this draft have been agreed in the Government Union on the one hand to introduce new taxes in order to obtain revenue for the budget. I am thinking of a mortgage tax, which has now been transformed into a new form, but also an advance payment by banks, a kilowatt-based tax on companies' cars, etc. On the other hand, this draft has introduced a change in the form of a more favourable taxation of participation options, which is not aimed at increasing the revenue of the state budget, but at a more favourable business environment. It is quite clear that, for the government, the problem of this draft or the underlying idea is that a large budget deficit needs to be dealt with. As regulations in different fields have been incorporated into a single draft, the MPs have been deprived of the opportunity to be in favour or against a specific proposal. For example, to support more favourable taxation of options, at the same time, greater taxation of banks or additional car taxation must also be supported. When the new leader of the Isamaa and Res Publica Union, Helir-Valdor Seeder, went to the government desk a few weeks ago to open a coalition agreement and talk about tax changes, it was his desire to restore the possibility of submitting joint declarations and to abolish the package excise duty and the system of withholding tax initiated by the government. It is now clear that none of these objectives were realised. There will be no return to joint declarations as of now. The enforcement of the bill on excise duties on packaging has been postponed, it has been put on hold nicely and when it will be adopted, it will perhaps become clear towards the autumn. The PIT system was replaced at the last minute by the obligation of the taxpayer to demonstrate whether the loan granted is a loan or a profit removal under that sign. We'll see how it works. In any case, the Group of the Reform Party also proposed the cancellation of the original version. The government's withdrawal of this initial version is certainly one of the few positive things about this draft. When the debate on the previous item ended by the end of the second reading of the bill on the tax on sweetened beverages, it essentially decided to make it sweeter. This amendment was prepared as early as last year under our leadership. This will motivate employees to contribute more to the company's development and to become more involved with the company. We also support the lower rate of 14% for the company's regular profit distributions provided for in the draft. As stated at the outset, this law affects many people, businesses and sectors. In our opinion, not all these changes should be dealt with at the same time. We support certain proposals, but do not support the draft as a whole. Thank you for your attention.

ParlaMint-EE_2017-06-14_U495	Honourable Riigikogu! We will begin the second reading of the Draft Amending Law 456 on the State Budget Act. The rapporteur for the Monetary Commission is Mikhail Stalnukhin, Chairman of the Committee. Please!
ParlaMint-EE_2017-06-14_U496	Mr. Chairman! This draft law was also launched on 4 May. The first reading took place on 17 May. In preparation for its second reading, the Committee on Finance discussed the draft at five meetings with representatives of the Ministry of Finance. The deadline for tabling amendments was 31 May. By this time, 39 amendments were received: 10 proposals from the Conservative People's Party of Estonia, 11 from the Free Party Group and 13 from the Estonian Reform Party Group, and 5 amendments came from the Special Committee on State Budget Control. The Committee on Finance decided to make six amendments to the draft, in cooperation with the Ministry of Finance, one of which consists essentially of eight related proposals. The explanations of the amendments are set out in the list of amendments to the proposals. Technical and linguistic improvements have been made to the text of the draft act. Quite a lot of work involved letters from the Commission. They came from the Supreme Court, the National Audit Office and the Estonian Chamber of Commerce and Industry; on 24 May, the Chancellor of Justice also sent a referral to the Monetary Affairs Committee. The Monetary Committee met representatives of Eesti Pank at its meeting on 2 May and representatives of the Fiscal Council on 8 May and took note of their information. Now I will talk about the proposals that have been made. The Supreme Court pointed out the problem that the draft seeks to exclude from the annual state budget law approved by the Riigikogu the level of breakdown of the areas of government of the ministries and to empower the Government of the Republic to decide on the allocation of state budget resources between the areas of government, so that the court administration council is no longer able to participate in the process of shaping the budget of the courts of first and second instance. In principle, the Committee on Monetary Affairs supported the amendments to the draft to ensure the independence of the judiciary. The National Audit Office forwarded to the Finance Committee a copy of the proposals sent to the Minister for Finance in the coordination process. The most important of these are the proposal to deal with the change in the budgetary position rule separately from the other changes which are due to enter into force during the budgetary procedure for 2018, as it was considered problematic. Reference was also made to a possible breach of the principle of separation and balance of powers, to the need to dismantle the state budget and draw up forecasts with sufficient clarity and to significantly improve the rules for the implementation and reporting of the state budget. The Committee on Monetary Affairs maintained the reasons put forward in this vast majority of the table of co-ordination. The Estonian Chamber of Commerce and Industry proposed that the draft provision (§ 1 (2)) be deleted, which allows the structural deficit of the budget to be shifted to the conclusion that the provision is not really necessary or justified in the long term and does not take account of any future economic problems. Of course, turning to the Chancellor of Justice proved to be the most effective. The Chancellor of Justice referred to a possible contradiction of the draft with the Constitution. According to the draft, the breakdown of the budget law disappears with the accuracy of the ministries, and these and lower-level articulation decisions fall within the competence of the Government of the Republic. According to the Chancellor of Justice, these provisions limit to an unacceptable extent the competence of the Riigikogu to make substantive decisions about the political priorities of the state next year. The Committee on Monetary Affairs decided to seek the opinion of the Committee on Constitutional Affairs on the possible constitutional contradictions set out in the letter of the Chancellor of Justice. This decision was taken on 30 May, but there was another accident at work because the Committee on Constitutional Affairs did not have a chair for a long time and the Committee on Constitutional Affairs did not deliver a reply until 12 June. In this reply, the Members of the Commission agreed with the Chancellor of Justice. They were of the opinion that the general role of the Riigikogu in the procedure of the budget must increase, the state budget should be made for years, and the Riigikogu must have the opportunity to decide on the budget with sufficient detail. The meeting of the Committee on Constitutional Affairs also pointed out that, following the submission of amendments, some revenue should not be left outside the scope of government and some programmes should not be directly government-owned. Following these opinions, the Committee on Monetary Affairs prepared amendments to the draft, including the draft to bring it into line with the Constitution. The changes preserve the inter-ministerial breakdown of the state budget, i.e. the provisions that the Riigikogu would have allocated funds to the Government of the Republic with the state budget and the government would have broken the funds between the ministries by decree. The principle of administrative breakdown is maintained and the obligation is restored that the Government of the Republic shall immediately submit the budget strategy to the Riigikogu after approval. It is stipulated that, as a general rule, the division between the areas of government of the ministries can only be amended by a change in the state budget. Furthermore, inaccuracies will be corrected and the entry into force provisions will be clarified. Together with these amendments, the Committee on Monetary Affairs took full, partial or substantive account of the 12 amendments tabled by the Riigikogu. These concerned the annual inclusion of planned external grants in the budget strategy, maintaining inter-ministerial breakdown in the state budget and giving the Government at least twice a year an overview of the use of state budget resources. The Finance Committee did not support proposals to amend the rules on budgetary positions and the deletion of §§ 35, 36 and 37 as a whole. In addition, the Committee on Monetary Affairs failed to take into account the proposals to set the deadline for drawing up estimates, as, depending on the availability of statistics, the forecast deadline may shift and there is no practical need to set a rigid date at the level of the law. The Commission failed to take into account the proposals to exclude from the law a provision which guarantees the constitutional institution, at law level, that the budget of the constitutional institution is to be broken down on an operational basis only on the initiative of the constitutional institution itself, not at the initiative of the Government of the Republic, as well as the proposals to delete the provision on the establishment of an efficiency plan, since the efficiency plan is a tool for the effective and efficient use of public resources, and also failed to take into account the proposal that the Government of the Republic should present the budget strategy for the approval of the Riigikogu, as the Chancellor of Justice considers that the approval of the basically valid medium-term budgetary framework would limit the rights of the next compositions and coalitions in the Riigikogu as problems arise with legitimate expectations. For my part, I was delighted by the Chancellor of Justice's address and the decision of the Committee on Constitutional Affairs. In my opinion, the changes made have improved the quality of this bill. The Committee on Finance took the following decisions: to put the draft on the agenda today, 14 June, and to propose that the second reading be closed. Thank you.

ParlaMint-EE_2017-06-14_U580	George Adams.
ParlaMint-EE_2017-06-14_U581	Mr President of the sitting! Ladies and gentlemen of the House! I will take this opportunity to say a few words on two subjects. The first is what we call the budgetary process, and the second is the problem that has already been repeatedly mentioned here, why it has not been possible to write into the Constitution that the budget should be balanced. On the first issue, I would like to say the following. At the beginning of the work of the IX Riigikogu it became clear that during the previous term the budget forecast had probably been too optimistic and based on it the budget. After Mart Laar's second government came to power, two negative state budgets had to be made during the first year and public spending had to be cut because the state's income was not sufficient at the time. One of the results of this was that a new draft of the basic law on the state budget was prepared at the Ministry of Finance and that law was also adopted in the Riigikogu. This has probably been changed in a number of ways later, but the result is that the opportunity for members of the Riigikogu to participate in the design of the budget is small, almost negligible, compared to the time before this reform. I do not know how this led to a change in the process of preparing the budget in the Ministry and the government institutions as a whole, but in any case the budget began to reach the members of the Riigikogu in a form that was not as clear as it was before, at least for people like me who were not specialists in this matter. I would therefore like to say that many of the amendments proposed in this draft are at the level of the idea, even if they are withdrawn, in fact the logical consequence of this new basic law, which was adopted in 2000 or 1999. We should probably find the roots of a lot of trouble there. The second thing is that, in its time, Europe really went through this, let's say, the modern idea that the principle of the balance of the state budget should be included in the Constitution. Some countries did. I had to deal with this problem a little at one time, and I also had to look at how it was done in other countries. I came to the conclusion that it may not be the case in every country, but in the Estonian country the attempt to write this thing in a sensible wording and a sensible length of constitution will probably never succeed. So when there were complaints that why the IRL had abandoned this idea, I think that those who promoted this idea had not thought through how it could be technically implemented. There are a wide range of problems here. If we have agreed that in this context we will address the balance in a certain sense, we would probably not be able to write it in one or two sentences, and if we wanted to write it very accurately, then perhaps we should include several pages of specific professional text in the Constitution, with a lot of foreign terms inside. More time, please!
ParlaMint-EE_2017-06-14_U582	Three more minutes.
ParlaMint-EE_2017-06-14_U583	This is not the only problem where we have the understanding that there should be something in the Constitution, but we cannot find an adequate verbal form suitable for our constitution. One such failed attempt is what I have also mentioned here and what you all know: in the chapter of the constitution of local governments there is a sentence that cities and municipalities are units of local government. But there are still a number of other things, questions of this degree of generalisation that cannot quite be put into concrete wording. So I call on us not to accuse the IRL too much of this. Accusation would mean that we would demand an unrealistic, unfeasible solution from them. This does not mean that we are abandoning the principle that our general understanding is that if there is no emergency situation, the state must not spend more than its normal incomes allow. Thank you for your attention.
